      minhash: k19vnQqdLlvqssoVbkgRgEHuM8zFTSg8NWZhRVJEhMYPZNuyNWge7xayLdpCKwEfOwAKTdnR2ubQO8i5IYoXStDnT4THyrDyq/qlHBvakQ+Qj7BDuDdS9wHb9GovOQhhW0Y+Gi78h/7E28rmJEupcbHGtW8FzRkpsS8sY5nIy2taGaq7GJysyLpvzfXStZt7/dVW5MyctzOiuv8ZrHRuXsF4J+0YNFSSWQ0BZ1cFeanENXMhZI2BXBem7Ni+a+feyQsil+yplkCt7ssVezh34EqdJjyuMCq7KzcS6RsZ1fWJCoCJDaK9Wh95WQ/Biw0JQyXUi04SrCusyqCnuBw1jw==

<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{1.1}
1) For $\ell < 6$, and cardinals $\lambda \ge \kappa$, $\theta$ and 
an ordinal $\alpha$, let\\ $\Pr^\ell_\alpha (\lambda;<\kappa,\theta)$ 
mean that for every model $M$ with the universe $\lambda$ and vocabulary of
cardinality $\le \theta$, $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;< \kappa) \ge \alpha$ 
(defined below) and let $\mathrm{NPr}^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;<\kappa,\theta)$ be 
the negation. Instead of  ``$<\kappa^+$" we may write $\kappa$ 
(similarly below); if $\kappa=\theta^+$ we may omit it (so e.g. 
$\Pr^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ means
$\Pr^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;<\kappa^+,\kappa)$); if $\theta = \aleph_0$ and 
$\kappa=\aleph_1$ we may omit them. 

Lastly, let $\lambda^\ell_\alpha(<\kappa,\theta) =
\min\{\lambda:\Pr^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;<\kappa,\theta)\}$. 

\noindent
2)  For a model $M$, 
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;<\kappa) = \sup\{\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa)+1:w \subseteq |M| 
\text{ finite non empty}\}$ where $\mathrm{rk}^\ell$ is defined below in part (3).

\noindent
3)  For a model $M$, and $w \in [M]^* := \{u:u \subseteq |M| 
\text{ is finite nonempty}\}$ we shall define below the truth value of
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa) \ge \alpha$ by induction on the ordinal 
$\alpha$ (note: if $c \ell_{<\kappa}(w,M) = c \ell_2(w,M)$ for every 
$w \in [M]^*$ then for $\ell=0,1$, $\kappa$ can be omitted). 

Then we can note:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[${(*)_0}$]  $\alpha \le \beta \text{ and } \mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa)
  \ge \beta \Rightarrow \mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa) \ge \alpha$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(*)_1$]  $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa) \ge \delta \;(\delta \text{
    limit}) \Leftrightarrow \bigwedge\limits_{\alpha< \delta} \mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<
  \kappa) \ge \alpha$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(*)_2$]  $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa) \ge 0 \Leftrightarrow w \in [M]^*$ and 
no $a \in w$ is in $c \ell_{<\kappa}(w \setminus \{a\},M)$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
So we can define $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa) = \alpha$ as the maximal 
$\alpha$ such that $\mathrm{rk}^\ell (w,M;<\kappa) \ge \alpha$, and $\infty$ if 
this holds for every $\alpha$ (and $-1$ whenever
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa) \not\geq 0$).

Now the inductive definition of $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\omega,M;< \kappa) \ge
\alpha$ was already done above for $\alpha=0$ (by $(*)_2$) and 
$\alpha$ limit (by $(*)_1$), so for $\alpha = \beta +1$ we let
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_3$]  $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\omega,M;< \kappa) \ge \beta +1$ \underline{iff} 
(letting $n=|w|$, $w=\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\}$) for every $k<n$ and a 
quantifier free formula $\varphi(x_0,\dots,x_{n-1}$) (in the
vocabulary of $M$) for which $M \models \varphi[a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}]$ we
have:
\newline
\underline{Case 1}: $\ell=1$.  There are $a^i_m \in M$ for $m<n,i<2$
such that:
\smallskip\noindent 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]  $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a^i_m:i<2,m<n\},M;<\kappa) \ge \beta$,
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(b)]  $M \models \varphi[a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1}]$ (for $i=1,2$), so 
without loss of generality there is no repetition in $a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1}$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(c)]  $a^0_k \ne a^1_k$ but for $m \ne k$ (such that $m<n$)  we have
$a^0_m=a^1_m$. 
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\underline{Case 2}: $\ell=0$. As for $\ell=1$ but in addition
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate} 
\item[(d)]  $\bigwedge\limits_{m} a_m=a^0_m$
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\underline{Case 3}:  $\ell=3$.  We give to $\kappa$ an additional role and the
definition is like case 1 but $i< \kappa$;  i.e. there are $a^i_m \in M$ for
$m<n,i < \kappa$ such that:
\medskip\noindent 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]  for $i<j < \kappa$ we have $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a^i_m,a^j_m:m<n\},
M;< \kappa) \ge \beta$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(b)]  $M \models \varphi[a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1}]$ \ (for $i <
  \kappa$; so without loss of generality there are no repetitions in
  $a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1}$)
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(c)]  for $i<j < \kappa,a^i_k \ne a^j_k$ but for $m \ne k$ 
(such that $m<n$) we have $\bigwedge\limits_{i,j<\kappa} a^i_m=a^j_m$  
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\underline{Case 4}:  $\ell=2$.  Like case 3 but in addition
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(d)]  $a_m=a^0_m$ for $m<n$
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\underline{Case 5}:  $\ell=5$.  Like case 3 except that we replace
clause (a) by
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)$^-$]  for every function $F$, $\mathrm{Dom}(F)=\kappa$, $|\mathrm{Rang}(F)|<\kappa$
for some $i<j<\kappa$ we have $F(i)=F(j)$ and 
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a^i_m,a^j_m:m<n\},M;< \kappa) \ge \beta$.
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\underline{Case 6}:   $\ell=4$. Like case 4 (i.e. $\ell=2$) using 
clause (a)$^-$ instead of clause (a). 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
\label{1.2}
1)  The truth of each of the statements of 
$\Pr^\ell_\alpha (\lambda;< \kappa,\theta)$, $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;< \kappa)
\ge \alpha$, $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w M;< \kappa) \ge \alpha$ is preserved if we replace
$\ell = 0,2,3,2,2,2,3,5,4$ by $\ell = 1,3,1,0,1,4,5$, $1,5$ respectively (i.e.
$2 \rightarrow 4$, $3 \rightarrow 5 \rightarrow 1$, $0 \rightarrow 1$, 
$2 \rightarrow 3$, $4 \rightarrow 5$, $3 \rightarrow 1$, $2 \rightarrow 0$, 
$2 \rightarrow 1$) and also if we decrease $\alpha,\kappa,\theta$ or 
increase $\lambda$ (the last two only when $M$ is not a parameter). 
So the corresponding
inequality on $\lambda^\ell_\alpha(< \kappa, \theta)$ holds.

\noindent
2)  Also $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w_1,M;< \kappa) \ge \mathrm{rk}^\ell(w_2,M;< \kappa)$ for 
$w_1 \subseteq w_2$ from $[M]^*$.  

\noindent
3)  Also if we expand $M$, the ranks (of $w \in [M]^*$, of $M$) can
only decrease. 

\noindent
4)  If $A \subseteq M$ is defined by a quantifier free formula with
parameters from a finite subset $w^*$ of $M$, 
$M^+$ is $M$ expanded by the relations defined by quantifier free
formulas with parameters from $w^*$, $M^* = M^+ \restriction A$ (for simplicity
$M^*$ has relations only) \underline{then} for $w \in [A]^*$ such that
$w \nsubseteq w^*$ we have $$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M^*;< \kappa) \ge 
\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w \cup w^*,M;< \kappa).$$   
Hence if $w^* = \varnothing$, $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M^*;< \kappa) \ge \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;<
\kappa)$.

\noindent
5)  In \ref{1.1}(3)$(*)_2$, if in the definition of $c \ell_{<\kappa}$ we
allow any first order formula, this means just
expanding $M$ by relations for any first order formula $\varphi(\bar
x)$.

\noindent
6)  For $\ell$ odd, $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;< \kappa) \ge (|\tau(M)|+\aleph_0)^+$ implies
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(u,M;<\kappa)=\infty$.

\noindent
7)  $\lambda^\ell_\alpha (<\kappa,\theta)$ increases ($\le$) with
$\alpha$, $\theta$ and decreases with $\kappa$.

\noindent
8) There is no difference between $\ell=4$ and $\ell=5$.
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
heck,                                               
[e.g. for part (8), we can use function $F$ such that
$(\forall \alpha<\kappa)(F(0) \ne F(1+\alpha)$].
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- claim-2: body_md -->
\label{1.3}
1) For $\ell=0$, if $\alpha = \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;< \kappa)$ $(<\infty)$ \underline{then}
for some expansion $M^+$ of $M$ by $\le \aleph_0+|\alpha|$ 
relations, for every $w \in [M]^*$ we have:  

\[
\mathrm{rk}^{\ell+1}(w,M^+;< \kappa) \le \mathrm{rk}^\ell (w,M;<\kappa).
\]

\medskip\noindent
2)  Similarly for $\ell=2,4$ .

\noindent
3)  If $\mathbf{V}_0$ is a transitive class of $\mathbf{V}_1$ (both models
of $\mathrm{ZFC}$) and $M \in \mathbf{V}_0$ is a model then:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]   for $\ell < 4$
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(\alpha)$]   $[\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;< \kappa)]^{\mathbf{V}_0} \le 
[\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;< \kappa)]^{\mathbf{V}_1}$ for $w \in [M]^*$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\beta)$]  $[\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;< \kappa)]^{\mathbf{V}_0} \le 
[\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;<\kappa)]^{\mathbf{V}_1}$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\gamma)$]   if $\ell=0,1$  equality holds in
  $(\alpha),(\beta)$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\delta)$]   $[\lambda^\ell_\alpha(\kappa)]^{\mathbf{V}_0} \le
[\lambda^\ell_\alpha(\kappa)]^{\mathbf{V}_1}$ if $\ell=0,1$.
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(b)]  Assume:
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)]   for every $f:\kappa \rightarrow \mathrm{Ord}$ from $\mathbf{V}_1$ there is
$A \in [\kappa]^\kappa$ such that $f \restriction A \in \mathbf{V}_0$, or at least
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(ii)]   every graph  $H$ on $\lambda$ from $\mathbf{V}_0$ which in 
$\mathbf{V}_1$ has a complete subgraph of size $\kappa$, has such a subgraph in
$\mathbf{V}_0$, which holds if
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(ii)$^+$]  $\mathbf{V}_1= \mathbf{V}_0^{\mathbf{P}}$ where $\mathbf{P}$ is a 
forcing notion satisfying the $\kappa$-Knaster Condition.
\underline{Then} for $\ell=2,3$, in $(\alpha),(\beta)$ (of (a)) above equalities
hold and the inequality in $(\delta)$ holds.
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(c)]  Assume $\mathbf{V}_1 = \mathbf{V}_0^\mathbf{P}$ where $\mathbf{P}$ 
is $\kappa-2$-linked.  \underline{Then} for $\ell=4,5$ in clauses
$(\alpha),(\beta)$ (of (a)) above we have equality and the 
inequality in $(\delta)$ holds.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end claim-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
) For $\beta < \alpha$, $n < \omega$, a quantifier free formula
$\varphi = \varphi(x_0,\ldots,x_{n-1})$ and $k<n$ let 

\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{clcr}
R_\beta^n = \{\langle a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle:& a_m \in M \text{ for } m<n
\text{ and} \\
  &\beta = \mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\},M;< \kappa)\},
\end{array}
\end{equation*}


\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{clcr}
R_{\beta,\varphi}^{n,k} = \{\langle a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle \in 
R^n_\beta: &M \models \varphi[a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}] \text{ for no} \\
   &a^1_k \in |M| \setminus\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\} \text{ we have} \\
  &(\alpha) \quad M \models
  \varphi[a_0,\dots,a_{k-1},a^1_k,a_{k+1},\dots,a_{n-1}] \\ 
  &(\beta) \quad \mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a_m:m<n\} \cup \{a^1_k\},M;< \kappa) \ge \beta\},
\end{array}
\end{equation*}

\[
M^+ = (M,\dots R^n_\beta,R^{n,k}_{\beta,\varphi} \ldots)_{\beta<\alpha,n<
  \omega,k<n,\varphi}
\]

\medskip\noindent
Check (or see more details in the proof of \ref{1.8} below).

\noindent
2) Similarly.

3) The proof should be clear (for (b), looking at Definition \ref{1.1} case
3 the graph is $\{(i,j)$: clause (a) there holds$\}$).
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{1.3A}
1)  In \ref{1.3}(1) we can omit ``$\alpha = \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;<\kappa)$" but
then weaken the conclusion to $\mathrm{rk}^{\ell+1}(w,M^+;<\kappa) \le 
\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;<\kappa)$ or both are $>\alpha$.

\noindent
2)  Similarly in \ref{1.3}(2).
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- conclusion-1: body_md -->
\label{1.4}
1)  $\Pr^0_{\omega_1}(\lambda) \Leftrightarrow \Pr^1_{\omega_1}(\lambda)
\Leftarrow \Pr^4_{\omega_1}(\lambda) \Leftrightarrow \Pr^5_{\omega_1}
(\lambda) \Leftarrow \Pr^2_{\omega_1}(\lambda) \Leftrightarrow
\Pr^3_{\omega_1}(\lambda)$.

\noindent
2)  If $\alpha \le \kappa^+$ then $\Pr^0_\alpha (\lambda;\kappa) 
\Leftrightarrow \Pr^1_\alpha (\lambda;\kappa) \Leftarrow 
\Pr^4_\alpha (\lambda;\kappa) \Leftrightarrow
\Pr^5_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa) \Leftarrow \Pr^2_\alpha (\lambda;\kappa)
\Leftrightarrow \Pr^3_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$. 

\noindent
3)  For $\alpha \le \kappa^+$, $\lambda^\ell_\alpha(\kappa) = 
\lambda^{\ell+1}_\alpha(\kappa)$ for $\ell=0,2,4$, and 
$\lambda^0_\alpha(\kappa) \le \lambda^4_\alpha(\kappa)\le 
\lambda^2_\alpha(\kappa)$.

\noindent
4)  For $\alpha \ge \kappa^+$ and $\ell=0,2,4$ we have
$\lambda^{\ell+1}_\alpha(\kappa)= \lambda^{\ell+1}_{\kappa^+} (\kappa)$.
<!-- end conclusion-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
) By 2).

\noindent
2) For $\alpha=\kappa^+$ it follows from its holding for every 
$\alpha < \kappa^+$. For $\alpha < \kappa^+$; for $\ell=0,2,4$ we know that
$\mathrm{NPr}^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa) \Rightarrow
\mathrm{NPr}^{\ell+1}_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ by \ref{1.3}(1),(2), and
$\Pr^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa) \Rightarrow
\Pr^{\ell+1}_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ by \ref{1.2}(1); together
$\Pr^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa) \Leftrightarrow
\Pr^{\ell+1}_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$. Now
$\Pr^3_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa) \Rightarrow \Pr^5_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)
\Rightarrow \Pr^1_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ by \ref{1.2}(1), together
we finish. (By \ref{2.1} we know more.)

\noindent
3) Follows from part (2) and the definition.

\noindent
4) By \ref{1.2}(6).
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- convention-1: body_md -->
\label{1.5}
Writing $\Pr_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ for $\alpha \le \kappa^+$ (omitting
$\ell$) we mean $\ell=0$.
Similarly $\lambda_\alpha(<\kappa,\theta)$ and so $\lambda_\alpha(\kappa)$ etc.
<!-- end convention-1 -->
<!-- claim-3: body_md -->
\label{1.6}
Let $\ell \in \{0,2,4\}$.

\noindent
1) $\mathrm{NPr}_{\alpha+1}^\ell (\kappa^{+\alpha};\kappa)$.

\noindent
2) If $\alpha$ is a limit ordinal $<\kappa^+$ (in fact, $\aleph_0 \le
\mathrm{cf}\!(\alpha)< \kappa^+$ suffice), and $\mathrm{NPr}_\beta (\lambda_\beta;\kappa)$ for
$\beta < \alpha$, \underline{then} $\mathrm{NPr}_{\alpha+1}^\ell (\sum_{\beta <
  \alpha} \lambda_\beta;\kappa)$.

\noindent
3) If $\mathrm{NPr}_\alpha^\ell (\lambda;\kappa)$ \underline{then}
$\mathrm{NPr}_{\alpha+1}^\ell 
(\lambda^+;\kappa)$.

\noindent
4) If $\mathrm{NPr}_\alpha^\ell (\mu;\kappa)$ for every $\mu < \lambda$ \underline{then}
$\mathrm{NPr}_{\alpha+1}^\ell (\lambda;\kappa)$.
<!-- end claim-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
) Prove by induction on $\alpha<\kappa^+$, for $\alpha=0$ use
a model in which 
every element is definable (e.g. an individual constant) so $\mathrm{rk}(w;M)=-1$
for $w \in [M]^*$ and hence $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M)=0$ and consequently 
$\mathrm{NPr}_1^\ell(\kappa;\kappa)$; for $\alpha$ limit use part (2) and for 
$\alpha$ successor use part (3). 

\noindent
2) Let $M_\beta$ witness $\mathrm{NPr}_\beta (\lambda_\beta;\kappa)$ for 
$\beta < \alpha$, i.e. $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M_\beta;\kappa) < \beta$ and
$M_\beta$ has universe $\lambda_\beta$ and
$|\tau(M_\beta)| \le \kappa$. Without loss of generality $\langle
\tau(M_\beta): \beta< \alpha\rangle$ are pairwise disjoint and disjoint
to $\{P_\beta: \beta< \alpha\}$.
Let $M$ have universe $\lambda := \sum\limits_{\beta <\alpha}
\lambda_\beta$, $P^M_\beta = \lambda_\beta$, and $M \restriction \lambda_\beta$ 
expand $M_\beta$ and $|\tau(M)| \le |\alpha|+ \sum\limits_{\beta \le \alpha}
|\tau(M_\beta)| \le \kappa$. 
By \ref{1.2}(3),(4), for $w \in [\lambda_\beta]^*$, 
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;\kappa)\le \mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M_\beta;\kappa)< \beta \le \alpha$. 
But $w \in\big[|M|\big]^*$ implies
$\bigvee\limits_{\beta < \alpha} w \in [\lambda_\beta]^*$. 
Clearly $\mathrm{rk}(M;\kappa) \le \alpha$ and hence
$\mathrm{NPr}_{\alpha+1}^\ell(\lambda;\kappa)$.

\noindent
3) We define $M^+$ such that each $\gamma \in [\lambda,\lambda^+)$
codes on $\{\zeta:\zeta < \gamma\}$ an example for $\mathrm{NPr}_\alpha
(|\gamma|;\kappa)$. More elaborately, let $M$ be a
model with the universe $\lambda$ such that $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;\kappa)<\alpha$. Let
$\tau(M)$ be $\{R_i:i < i^* \le \kappa\},R_i$ an $n(i)$-place 
predicate (as we replace function symbols and individual constants 
by predicates), $R_0$ is a 0-nary
predicate representing ``the truth''. For $\gamma\in [\lambda,\lambda^+)$
let $f_\gamma$ be a one-to-one function from $\gamma$ onto $\lambda$. Define
$\tau^+=\{R_i,Q_i : i < i^* \le \kappa\}$, $R_i$ is $n(i)$-place, $Q_i$ is
$(n(i)+1)$-place. So $|\tau^+| \le \kappa$. We define a $\tau^+$-model $M^+$:
the universe is $\lambda^+$, $R^{M^+}_i=R^M_i$,
$Q^{M^+}_i = \{\langle\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_{n(i)}\rangle: \alpha_{n(i)}\in
[\lambda,\lambda^+)$ and $\bigwedge\limits_{\ell < n(i)}
\alpha_\ell < \alpha_{n(i)}$ and $\langle f_{\alpha_{n(i)}}(\alpha_0),
\ldots,f_{\alpha_{n(i)}}(\alpha_{n(i)-1})\rangle \in R^M_i\}$
(so $Q_0^{M^+}=[\lambda,\lambda^+)$). 

Now note that:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]  for $w \in[\lambda]^*$, 
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M^+;\kappa) \le \mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M; \kappa)$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(b)]  if $w \subseteq \gamma\in [\lambda,\lambda^+)$, $w \ne \varnothing$
then $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w \cup\{\gamma\},M^+;\kappa) \le
\mathrm{rk}^\ell(f_\gamma''[w],M;\kappa)$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
(Easy to check).  So if $\gamma<\lambda^+$ then
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_1$]  $\gamma < \lambda \Rightarrow \mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{\gamma\},M^+;
\kappa) \le \mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{\gamma\},M;\kappa) < \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;\kappa)$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(*)_2$]  $\gamma \in[\lambda,\lambda^+)\ \&\ \beta \ge \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;
\kappa)\ \Rightarrow\ \mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{\gamma\},M^+; \kappa) \le \beta$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
[Why $(*)_2$?  Assume not and let $\kappa^0=2$, $\kappa^2=\kappa^4=\kappa^+$.
If $\langle \gamma_i: i< \kappa^l\rangle$ strictly increasing witnesses
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{\gamma\}, M^+) \ge \beta+1$ for the formula
$Q_0(x)$ then for some $i< j < \kappa^l$ we have
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{\gamma_i,\gamma_j\}, M^+) \ge \beta$ and applying (b)
with $\{\gamma_i\}$, $\gamma_j$ here standing for $w$, $\gamma$ there
we get $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{f_{\gamma_j}(\gamma_i)\},M) \ge \beta$ hence
$\beta+1 \le \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M)$, contradiction.]

Hence
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_3$]  $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M^+;\kappa) \le \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M; \kappa)+1$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
As $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M^+; \kappa)< \alpha$ clearly $M^+$ witnesses
$\mathrm{NPr}_{\alpha+1}(\lambda^+; \kappa)$.

\noindent
4) Like (3).
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- conclusion-2: body_md -->
\label{1.7}
Remembering that $\lambda_\alpha (\kappa)=\min\{\lambda: \Pr_\alpha
(\lambda; \kappa)\}$ we have: 
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item  for $\alpha$ a limit ordinal $\lambda_\alpha (\kappa) \le 
\beth_{\alpha}(\kappa)$ and even $\lambda^2_\alpha(\kappa) \le  
\beth_{\alpha}(\kappa)$
\smallskip\noindent
\item  for $\ell$ even $\langle \lambda_\alpha^\ell(\kappa):0 <
\alpha<\infty\rangle$ is strictly increasing, and for
a limit ordinal $\delta$, $\lambda_\delta(\kappa) = 
\sup\limits_{\alpha<\delta} \lambda_\alpha(\kappa)$
\smallskip\noindent
\item  $\lambda_0(\kappa) = \lambda_1(\kappa)=\kappa$, 
$\lambda_2(\kappa) = \kappa^+$, 
$\kappa^{+n} \le \lambda_n(\kappa )< \kappa^{+ \omega}$ 
and $\lambda_\omega(\kappa) = \kappa^{+ \omega}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end conclusion-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{1.7A}
[[Saharon $\lambda^2_{\omega\times\alpha} (\kappa) \le 
\beth_{\omega \times \alpha}(\kappa)$]]
$\lambda^2_{\omega\times \alpha} (\kappa) \le \beth_{\omega\times \alpha}
(\kappa)$ is proved below essentially like the Morley 
omitting types theorem (see \cite{Mo} or see 
  \cite{CK73}                                      
or 
\cite[Ch.VII,\S5]{Sh:a} = \cite[Ch.VII,\S5]{Sh:c}).
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
) We prove by induction on $\alpha$, that {\it for every} ordinal 
$\beta<\alpha$, model $M$, $|\tau(M)| \le \kappa$, and $A \subseteq|M|$,
$|A|\ge \beth_{\omega\times\alpha}(\kappa)$, and $m$, $n<\omega$ 
{\it there is} $w \subseteq A$, $|w|=n$ such that 
$\mathrm{rk}^2(w,M;\kappa)\ge \omega\times\beta+m$. 

For $\alpha=0$, $\alpha$ limit this is immediate.
For $\alpha= \gamma+1$ (and $M$, $A$, $\beta$, $n$, $m$ as above),
applying Erd\H{o}s-Rado theorem we can find distinct $a_i\in A$ for
$i<\beth_{\omega\times\gamma}(\kappa)^{++}$ such that:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]  for all $i_0< \ldots <i_{m+n}$ the quantifier free type
$\langle a_{i_0},\ldots,a_{i_{n+m}}\rangle$ in $M$ is the same
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(b)]  for each $k \le m+n$, for every $i_0 < \ldots <
i_{n+m-k}<\beth_{\omega\times\gamma}(\kappa)$, the ordinal
$\min\{\omega\times\alpha,\mathrm{rk}^2(\{a_{i_0},\ldots,a_{i_{n+m-k}}\},M;
\kappa)\}$ is the same.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
By the induction hypothesis, in clause (b) the value is $\ge
\omega\times \gamma$. Hence we can prove, by induction on $k \le m+n$,
that $\mathrm{rk}^2(\{a_{i_0},\ldots,a_{i_{n+m-k}}\},M;\kappa) \ge
\omega\times\gamma+k$ whenever $i_0<\ldots < i_{m+n-k} <
\beth_{\omega\times\gamma}(\kappa)$. For $k=0$ this holds by
the previous sentence, for $k+1$ use the definition and the induction
hypothesis, for $\mathrm{rk}^2$ note that by clause (b) without loss of generality $i_\ell +
\kappa^+ < i_{\ell+1}$ and $a_{i_\ell +\zeta}$ for $\zeta< \kappa^+$ are
well defined. For $k=m$ we are done.

\noindent
2) It is increasing by \ref{1.2}(1), strict by \ref{1.6}(4),
continuous because, for limit $\delta$, as on the one hand
$\lambda^\ell_\delta(\kappa) \ge \sup\limits_{\alpha< \delta}
\lambda^\ell_\alpha (\kappa)$ as $\lambda^l_\delta(\kappa)\geq
\lambda^\ell_\alpha(\kappa)$ for $\alpha< \delta$, and on the other hand if
$M$ is a model with universe $\lambda := \sup\limits_{\alpha< \delta}
\lambda_\alpha(\kappa)$ and $|\tau(M)| \le \kappa$ then $\alpha< \delta
\Rightarrow \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;\kappa) \ge \mathrm{rk}^\ell(M\restriction \lambda_\alpha;
\kappa) \ge \alpha$ hence $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;\kappa) \ge \delta$. So
$\Pr_\alpha(\lambda; \kappa)$ hence 
$\lambda \ge \lambda^\ell_\delta(\kappa)$ so 
$\sup\limits_{\alpha<\delta}\lambda^l_\alpha(\kappa)= 
\lambda \ge \lambda^l_\delta$, together we are done.

\noindent
3) By \cite{Sh:49} (for the last two clauses, the first two clauses
are trivial), will not be really used here.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- claim-4: body_md -->
\label{1.8}
1)  Assume $\mathbf{P}$ is a forcing notion satisfying the $\kappa^+-c.c.$. If
$\Pr^3_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ and $\alpha \le \kappa^+$, then this 
holds in $\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$ too.

\noindent
2) If $\mathbf{P}$ is a $\kappa^+-2$-linked forcing notion (or just: if $p_i\in
\mathbf{P}$ for $i< \kappa^+$ then for some $F: \kappa^+ \to \kappa$,
$F(i)=F(j) \Rightarrow p_i,\ p_j$ compatible),  and $\alpha \le \kappa^+$ and
$\Pr^5_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ {\it then} this holds in $\mathbf
V^\mathbf{P}$ too.
<!-- end claim-4 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{1.8A}
1)  $\mathrm{NPr}_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ is of course preserved by 
any extension as the ranks $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M;\kappa)$,
$\mathrm{rk}^\ell(w,M;\kappa)$ are absolute for $\ell=0,1$ (see 
  \ref{1.3}(3)).                                                 
But  the forcing can add new models.

\noindent
2) So for $\alpha \le \kappa^+$, $\lambda_\alpha(\kappa) \le 
\lambda^4_\alpha(\kappa) \le \lambda^2_\alpha(\kappa)$ and a $\kappa^+$-c.c.
forcing notion can only increase the first (by 
                                             
  \ref{1.3}(3)(a)$(\delta)$)
and decrease the third by \ref{1.8}(1); a
$\kappa^+-2$-linked one fixes the second and third (as it can only
decrease it by \ref{1.8}(1) and can only increase it by
                                                         
  \ref{1.3}(3)(c)(a)$(\delta)$ + (b)$(\gamma)$).

\noindent
3) [[Of course]] We can deal similarly with $\Pr^\ell_\alpha(\lambda;
<\kappa, \theta)$, here and in                                   
\ref{1.3} -- \ref{1.7}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
e can concentrate on 1), anyhow let $\ell=\{3,5\}$ (for part
(1) we use $\ell=3$, for part (2) we shall use $\ell=5$,
we shall return to it later). Assume $\Pr^3_\alpha(\lambda;\kappa)$ fails in
$\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$. So for some $p^*\in \mathbf{P}$ and 
$\alpha_0<\alpha$ we have:

\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{clcr}
p^* \Vdash_\mathbf{P} &``\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \text{ is a model with universe } \lambda, 
\text{ vocabulary } \let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\tau$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \\
  &\text{ of cardinality } \le \kappa \text{ and } 
\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa) = \alpha_0."
\end{array}
\end{equation*}

\medskip\noindent
Without loss of generality, every quantifier free formula $\varphi(x_0,\dots,x_{n-1})$ 
is equivalent to one of the form $R(x_0,\dots,x_{n-1})$ and without loss of generality 
$\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\tau$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} = \{R_{n,\zeta}: n< \omega,\zeta < \kappa\}$ with 
$R_{n,\zeta}$ an $n$-place predicate. Note
that necessarily $\alpha_0< \kappa^+$ hence $|\alpha_0|\leq \kappa$.

As we can replace $\mathbf{P}$ by $\mathbf{P} \restriction \{q \in \mathbf{P}:p^* \le
q\}$, without loss of generality $p^*$ is the minimal member of {$\mathbf{P}$}.  
Now for non zero $n< \omega$, $k < n$, $\zeta < \kappa$ and $\beta < \alpha_0$ 
(or $\beta=-1$) we define an $n$-place relation 
$R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k}$ on $\lambda$:  

\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{clcr}
R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k} = \{&\langle a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\rangle:  a_m \in
\lambda \text{ with no repetitions and for some}\\
 &p \in \mathbf{P},\\
 &p \Vdash_\mathbf{P} ``\large[\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \models R_{n,\zeta}[a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}]
 \text{ and } \mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\},\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa) = \beta,\\
 &\quad \text{ where ``not } \mathrm{rk}^3(\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\},M;\kappa) \ge 
\beta +1" \\
  &\quad \text{ is witnessed by } \varphi = R_{n,\zeta} 
\text{ and } k\large]"\}.
\end{array}
\end{equation*}

\medskip\noindent
Let $M^+ = 
(\lambda,\dots,R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k},\dots)_{n<\omega,\zeta<\kappa,
\beta<\alpha_0, k<n}$, so $M^+$ is a model in $\mathbf{V}$ with the 
universe $\lambda$ and the vocabulary of
cardinality $\le \kappa$. It suffices to prove that for $\beta <
\alpha_0$: 
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$\otimes_\beta$]   if $w = \{a_0,\ldots,a_{n-1}\} \in [M^+]^*$,
$M^+ \models R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k} [a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}]$\\
then $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\},\;M^+;\kappa) \le \beta$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
(Note that by the choice of $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ and $R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k}$, if $w\in
[M^+]^*$ then for some $n,\zeta,\beta,k$ we have $M^+\models
R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k}[a_0,\ldots,a_{n-1}]$). 
This we prove by induction on $\beta$, so assume the conclusion fails; so
\[\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}\},M^+;\kappa) \ge \beta+1\]
(and eventually we shall get a contradiction).  
By the definition of $\mathrm{rk}^3$ applied to $\varphi =
R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k},\beta$ and $k$ we know that there are $a^i_m$ (for
$m<n,i< \kappa^+$) as in Definition \ref{1.1}(3) case $\ell=3$.
In particular $M^+\models R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k}[a^i_0,\ldots, a^i_{n-1}]$.
So for each $i<\kappa^+$ by the definition of $R_{n,\zeta,\beta,k}$
necessarily there is $p_i\in\mathbf{P}$ such that
$p_i \Vdash_{\mathbf{P}} ``\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \models R_{n,\zeta}[a_0^i,\dots,
a_{n-1}^i]$ and $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(\{a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1}\},\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa) =\beta$ and
[not $\mathrm{rk}^\ell (\{a_0^i,\dots,a_{n-1}^i\},\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa) \ge \beta
+1]$ is witnessed by $\varphi = R_{n,\zeta}$ and $k$". 

For part (1), as $\mathbf{P}$ satisfies the $\kappa^+-cc$, for some 
$q \in \mathbf{P},q \Vdash ``\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} = \{i:p_i \in \let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3G$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\mathbf{P}\}$ 
has cardinality $\kappa^+$" (in fact, $p_i$ forces it for every 
large enough $i$).  Looking at the definition of the rank in 
$\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$ we see that $\langle\langle a^i_0,\ldots,
a^i_{n-1}\rangle:i\in\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \rangle$ cannot
be a witness for ``the demand
for $\mathrm{rk}^3(\{a_0^{i_0},\ldots,a_{n-1}^{i_0}\},\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa)> \beta$ 
for $R_{n,\zeta,k}$ hold" for any (or some) $i_0 \in \let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$, so for part (1)
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(*)]   $q \Vdash_\mathbf{P}$ ``for some $i \ne j$ in $\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ we have
$\mathrm{rk}^3(\{a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k\},\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa) < \beta"$
\newline
(as the demand on equalities holds trivially).
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent 
As we can increase $q$, without loss of generality $q$ forces a value to those $i,j$, 
hence without loss of generality for some $n(*)=n+1 < \omega,\zeta(*)< \kappa$ and 
$\beta(*) < \beta$ and for $k(*)<n+1$ we have 

\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{clcr}
q \Vdash_\mathbf{P} ``&\mathrm{rk}^3(\{a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1}, a^j_k\},\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa) =\beta(*), \text{ and}\\ 
   &\mathrm{rk}^3(\{a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k\},M;\kappa )\not\ge 
\beta(*)+1 \text{ is witnessed by}\\ 
   &\varphi = R_{n(*),\zeta(*)}(x_0,\dots,x_n) \text{ and } k(*)".
\end{array}
\end{equation*}

\medskip\noindent
Hence by the definition of $R_{n(*),\zeta(*),\beta(*),k(*)}$ we have 

\[
M^+ \models
R_{n(*),\zeta(*),\beta(*),k(*)}[a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k].
\]

\medskip\noindent
As $\beta(*) < \beta$ by the induction hypothesis 
$\otimes_{\beta(*)}$ holds hence

\[
\mathrm{rk}^3(\{a^i_0,\dots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k\},M^+;\kappa) \le \beta(*),
\] 

\medskip\noindent
but this contradicts the choice of $a^i_m(m<n,\; i<\kappa^+)$ above
(i.e. clause (a) of Definition \ref{1.1}(3) case $\ell=3$). This contradiction
finishes the induction step in the proof of $\otimes_\beta$ hence the proof
of \ref{1.8}(1).

For part (2), we have $\langle p_i: i< \kappa^+\rangle$ as above.
In $\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$, if $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}=\{i: p_i\in G_\mathbf{P}\}$ has cardinality
$\kappa^+$, then $\langle \langle a_0^i,\ldots,a^i_{n-1}\rangle:i \in
\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \rangle$ cannot witness $\mathrm{rk}^5(\{a_0,\ldots,a_{n-1}\},M;
\kappa) \ge \beta+1$ so there is a function $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3F$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}^0: \let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}
\rightarrow \kappa$ witnessing it; i.e.
$\Vdash_\mathbf{P}$ ``if $|\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}|=\kappa^+$ then
$i,j \in \let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$, $i \ne j$ and $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3F$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}^0(i)=
\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3F$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}^0(j) \Rightarrow \beta > \mathrm{rk}^5(\{a^i_0,\ldots,a^i_{n-1}\}
\cup\{a^j_0,\ldots,a^j_{n-1}\},M;\kappa)"$.

If $|\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}| \le \kappa$, let
$\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3F$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}^0: \let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth} \rightarrow \kappa$ be one to one.
Let $p_i \le q_i \in \mathbf{P},q_i \Vdash \let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3F$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}^0(i)=\gamma_i$. As
$\mathbf{P}$ is $\kappa^+$-2-linked, for some function $F^1: \kappa^+ \rightarrow
\kappa$ we have $(\forall i, j < \kappa^+) (F^1(i)= F^1(j) \Rightarrow
q_i, q_j$ are compatible in $\mathbf{P}$). We now define a
function $F$ from $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3Y$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ to $\kappa$ by $F(i) =\mathrm{pr} (\gamma_i,
F^1(i))$ (you can use any pairing function $\mathrm{pr}$ on
$\kappa$). So if $i< j< \kappa^+$ and $F(i)=F(j)$ then there is $q_{i,j}$
such that $\mathbf{P} \models ``q_i \le q_{i,j}$ and $q_j \le q_{i,j}"$, 
hence $q_{i,j} \Vdash_\mathbf{P}
``\mathrm{rk}^5(\{a^i_0,\ldots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k\},
\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};\kappa)< \beta$", so possibly increasing $q_{i,j}$, for some 
$\beta_{i,j} < \beta$ and $\zeta_{i,j} < \kappa$ and $k_{i,j} < n$ 
we have $q_{i,j} \Vdash
``\mathrm{rk}^5(\{a^i_0,\ldots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k\},\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3M$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth};
\kappa)=\beta_{i,j}$ and $\mathrm{rk}^5(\{a^i_0,\ldots,a^i_{n-1},a^j_k\})
\ngeq \beta_{i,j}+1$ is witnessed by
$\varphi=R_{n+1,\zeta_{i,1}}(x_0,\ldots,x_n)$ and $k_{i,j}$".

Hence by the definition of $R_{n+1, \zeta_{i, j}, \beta_{i, j},
k_{i,j}}$ we have 

\[
M^+ \models R_{n+1,\zeta_{i,j},\beta_{i,j},k_{i,j}}[a^i_0,\ldots, 
a^i_{n-1},a^j_k],
\]

\medskip\noindent
but $\beta_{i,j} < \beta$ hence by the induction hypothesis 

\[
\mathrm{rk}^5(\{a^i_0,\ldots,a^j_{n-1},a^j_k\},M^+;\kappa) \le \beta_{i,j}.
\]

\medskip\noindent
So $F$ contradicts the choice of $\langle
\langle a^i_0,\ldots,a^i_{n-1}\rangle:i< \kappa^+\rangle$ i.e. clause
(a)$^-$ of Definition \ref{1.1} Case 5.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- claim-5: body_md -->
\label{1.9}
Let $B \subseteq {}^\omega 2 \times {}^\omega 2$ be a Borel or 
even analytic set and $\Pr_{\omega_1}(\lambda)$. 

\noindent
1)  If $B$ contains a $\lambda$-square \underline{then} $B$ contains a perfect
square.

\noindent
2) If $B$ contains a $(\lambda,\lambda)$-rectangle \underline{then} $B$
contains a perfect rectangle. 

\noindent
3) We can replace analytic by $\kappa$-Souslin if $\Pr_{\kappa^+} 
(\lambda;\kappa)$.  
(This applies to $\Sigma^1_2$ sets which are $\aleph_1$-Souslin).
<!-- end claim-5 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
ou can apply the results of section 2 to prove \ref{1.9};
specifically \ref{2.1} $(1)\Rightarrow(2)$ proves parts (1),(2)
and \ref{2.3}(1) proves part 3. of \ref{1.9}; those results of \S2 say
more hence their proof should be clearer.

However, we give a proof of part (1) here for the reader who is
going to read this section only. Suppose that $B \subseteq {}^\omega 2
\times {}^\omega 2$ is a Borel or even analytic set containing a 
$\lambda$-square. Let $T$ be a $(2,2,\omega)$-tree such that

\[
B = \big\{(\eta_0,\eta_1)\in {}^\omega                                
{2} \times {}^\omega 2:
(\exists\rho \in {}^\omega \omega)\big[(\eta_0,\eta_1,\rho) \in \lim
(T)\big]\big\},
\]

\medskip\noindent
and let $\{\eta_\alpha:\alpha<\lambda\} \subseteq {}^\omega 2$ be 
such that the square determined by it is contained in $B$ and 
$\alpha<\beta<\lambda \Rightarrow \eta_\alpha \ne \eta_\beta$. 
For $\alpha,\beta <\lambda$ let $F(\alpha,\beta) \in {}^\omega \omega$ 
be such that $(\eta_\alpha,\eta_\beta,F(\alpha,\beta)) \in \lim(T)$.
Define a model $M$ with the universe $\lambda$ and the vocabulary
$\tau = \{R_{\nu_0,\nu_1,\nu},Q_{\nu_0,\nu}:\nu_0,\nu_1 \in {}^{\omega >}2$
and $\nu\in {}^{\omega >} \omega\}$, each $R_{\nu_0,\nu_1,\nu}$ a binary
predicate, $Q_{\nu_0,\nu}$ a unary predicate and

\[
Q^M_{\nu_0,\nu} = \{\alpha<\lambda:\nu_0\triangleleft \eta_\alpha
\text{ and } \nu \triangleleft F(\alpha,\alpha)\},
\] 

\[
R_{\nu_0,\nu_1,\nu}^M = \{(\alpha,\beta) \in \lambda \times \lambda:
\nu_0 \triangleleft \eta_\alpha \text{ and } \nu_1 \triangleleft
\eta_\beta \text{ and } \nu \triangleleft F(\alpha,\beta)\}.
\] 

\medskip\noindent
By $\Pr_{\omega_1}(\lambda)$ we know that $\mathrm{rk}^0(M) \ge \omega_1$. 

A pair $(u,h)$ is called {\em an $n$-approximation} if $u\subseteq {}^n2$,
$h: u\times u\rightarrow {}^n \omega$ and for every 
$\gamma < \omega_1$ there is $w \in [\lambda]^*$ such that:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(\oplus_1)$]  $u = \{\eta_\alpha\restriction n:\alpha\in w\}$ 
and $\eta_\alpha\restriction n \ne \eta_\beta \restriction n$ for distinct
$\alpha,\beta \in w$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\oplus_2)$]  $\mathrm{rk}^0(w,M) \ge \gamma$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\oplus_3)$]  $F(\alpha,\beta)\restriction n=
h(\eta_\alpha\restriction n,\eta_\beta \restriction n)$ for
$\alpha,\beta \in w$; hence 
\[
M \models R_{\eta_\alpha\restriction n,\eta_\beta \restriction n,
h(\eta_\alpha\restriction n,\eta_\beta \restriction n)}[\alpha,\beta]
\] 
for $\alpha,\beta \in w$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
Note that $(\{\langle\ \rangle\},\{((\langle\ \rangle,\langle\ \rangle),
\langle\ \rangle)\})$ is a 0-approximation. 

Moreover
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_0$]  if $(u,h)$ is an $n$-approximation and $\nu^* \in u$
\underline{then} there are $m>n$ and an $m$-approximation $(u^+,h^+)$ such
that: 
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)]   $\nu \in u\setminus \{\nu^*\}\ \Rightarrow\ (\exists!
\nu^+)(\nu\triangleleft \nu^+\in u^+)$, 
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(ii)]   $(\exists^{!2}\nu^+)(\nu^*\triangleleft \nu^+\in u^+)$ 
(where $\exists^{!2} x$ means ``there are exactly $2$ $x$'s)
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(iii)]  $\nu \in u^+\ \Rightarrow\ \nu\restriction n\in u$ and
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(iv)]   if $\nu_1,\nu_2 \in u^+$ then $[h(\nu_1 \restriction
  n,\nu_2\restriction n) \triangleleft h^+(\nu_1,\nu_2)$ or 
$(\nu_1 \restriction n=\nu_2\restriction n=\nu^*$ and $\nu_1 \ne \nu_2)]$. 
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
[Why?  For each $\gamma < \omega_1$ choose $w_\gamma$ satisfying $(\oplus_1)$,
$(\oplus_2)$ and $(\oplus_3)$ for $\gamma+1$, now apply the definition
of $\mathrm{rk}^0$ (if $w_\gamma=\{\alpha^\gamma_\ell:\ell < |w_\gamma|\}$,
$\nu^*\triangleleft \eta_{\alpha^\gamma_k}$, $k<|w_\gamma|$ we apply
it to $k$) to get $w^+_\gamma= w_\gamma\cup \{\alpha_\gamma\}$
satisfying $(\oplus_1)$, $(\oplus_2)$ and $(\oplus_3)$ for $\gamma$,
then choose $m_\gamma\in (n, \omega)$ such that $\langle \eta_\alpha
\restriction m_\gamma: \alpha\in w^+_\gamma\rangle$ is with no
repetitions. 

Lastly, as there are only countably many possibilities for 
$$\big\langle m_\gamma,
\{\eta_\alpha\restriction m_\gamma: \alpha\in w^+_\gamma\},\ 
\{(\eta_\alpha\restriction m_\gamma, \eta_\beta\restriction m_\gamma,
F(\alpha, \beta)\restriction m_\gamma): \alpha,\beta \in w^+_\gamma\}\big\rangle$$
for $\gamma<\omega_1$, so one value is obtained for uncountably many
$\gamma$. Let $\gamma^*$ be one of them. Choose $m=m_{\gamma^*}$,
$u^+=\{\eta_\alpha \restriction m: \alpha\in w^+_\gamma\}$ and define
$h^+$ to satisfy $(\oplus_3)$.]

Repeating $|u|$-times the procedure of $(*)_0$ we get:
\medskip\noindent 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_1$]   if $u = \{\nu_\ell:\ell< k\} \subseteq {}^n2$ (no repetition),
$(u,h)$ is an $n$-approximation, then there are $m,u^+ =
\{\nu_\ell^+:\ell< 2k\}$ and $h^+$ such that $(u^+,h^+)$ is an 
$m$-approximation for some $m>n$ and
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)]  $\nu_l\triangleleft \nu_{2l}^+$, $\nu_\ell \triangleleft 
\nu_{2\ell+ 1}^+$, $\nu_{2 \ell}^+ \ne \nu_{2\ell+1}^+$,
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(ii)]  if $\ell<k$, $i<2$ then $h(\nu_\ell,\nu_\ell) \triangleleft
h^+(\nu_{2 \ell+i}^+,\nu_{2 \ell+i}^+)$ and
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(iii)]  if $\ell_1 \ne \ell_2$, $\ell_1,\ell_2 < k$ and 
$i,j<2$ then $h(\nu_{\ell_1},\nu_{\ell_2}) \triangleleft 
h^+(\nu_{2 \ell_1+i}^+,\nu_{2 \ell_2+j}^+)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
Consequently we have:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_2$]   there are sequences $\langle n_i: i<\omega\rangle\subseteq
\omega$ and $\langle (u_i,h_i):i \in \omega \rangle$ such that $n_i<n_{i+1}$,
$(u_i,h_i)$ is an $n_i$-approximation and $(u_i,h_i),(u_{i+1},h_{i+1})$
are like $(u,h),(u^+,h^+)$ of $(*)_1$. 
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
Now, let $\langle n_i : i < \omega \rangle$ and $\langle (u_i,h_i):
i \in \omega \rangle$ be as in $(*)_2$.  Define 

\[
\mathscr{P} = \big\{\eta \in {}^\omega 2: (\forall i \in \omega)
[\eta\restriction n_i\in u_i]\big\}.
\]

\medskip\noindent
By $(*)_1$ for $(u_{i+1}, h_{i+1})$ we know that ${\mathscr{P}}$ is a perfect
set. We claim that ${\mathscr{P}} \times {\mathscr{P}} \subseteq B$. Suppose that
$\eta',\eta''\in {\mathscr{P}}$ and $\eta' \ne \eta''$.
Then $\eta'\restriction n_{i(*)} \neq \eta''\restriction n_{i(*)}$ for some $i(*) < \omega$
and the sequence $\langle h_i(\eta'\restriction n_i,\eta''\restriction n_i):i(*) \le
i< \omega \rangle$ is $\triangleleft$-increasing and (as 
$(u_i,h_i)$ are approximations) $(\eta'\restriction n_i,\eta''\restriction n_i,
h_i(\eta'\restriction n_i,\eta''\restriction n_i)) \in T$ is increasing for 
$i\in [i(*),\omega)$. The case $\eta'=\eta'' \in {\mathscr{P}}$ is
easier. The claim is proved.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{1.10}
Assume $\mathrm{NPr}_{\omega_1} (\lambda)$ and $\lambda \le \mu=
\mu^{\aleph_0}$. \underline{Then} for some $c.c.c.$ forcing notion $\mathbf{P}$, 
$|\mathbf{P}|=\mu$ and $\Vdash_\mathbf{P}``2^{\aleph_0}=\mu$" 
and in $\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$ we have:   
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)$]   there is a Borel set $B \subseteq {}^\omega 2 \times
  {}^\omega 2$ such that:
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]   It contains a $\lambda$-square: i.e. there are pairwise distinct
$\eta_\alpha \in {}^\omega 2$ for $\alpha < \lambda$ such that 
$(\eta_\alpha,\eta_\beta)\in B$ for $\alpha,\beta <\lambda$.  
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(b)]   Let $\mathbf{V} \models \lambda^{\aleph_0} = \lambda_1$. $B$ contains no
$\lambda_1^+$-square, i.e. there are no $\eta_\alpha \in {}^\omega 2$ 
(for $\alpha < \lambda^+_1$) such that
$[\alpha \neq \beta \Rightarrow \eta_\alpha \ne \eta_\beta]$ 
and $(\eta_\alpha,\eta_\beta) \in B$
for $\alpha,\beta < \lambda^+$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(c)]   $B$ contains no perfect square.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
Actually $B$ is a countable union of closed sets.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
{Stage A}:   Clearly for some $\alpha(*) < \omega_1$ we have
$\mathrm{NPr}^1_{\alpha(*)}(\lambda)$. 
Let $M$ be a model with universe $\lambda$ and a countable vocabulary
such that $\mathrm{rk}^1(M)< \alpha(*)$ say with $<^\mu$ the usual order. 
Let functions $\varphi^M,k^M$ with domains 

\[
[\lambda]^* = \{u:u \subseteq \lambda,\ u \text{ is finite and } u \ne 
\varnothing\}
\]

\medskip\noindent
be such that: if $u=\{\alpha_0,\dots,\alpha_{n-1}\} \in [\lambda]^*$ 
increasing for definiteness, $\beta = \mathrm{rk}^0(u,M)$
($<\alpha(*)$) \underline{then} $\varphi^M(u)$ is a quantifier free formula in the 
vocabulary of $M$ in the variables $x_0,\ldots,x_{n-1}$ for simplicity saying 
$x_0<x_1<\ldots < x_{n-1}$, $k^M(u)$ is a natural number $< n=|u|$
such that $\varphi^M(u)$, $k^M(u)$ witness ``not $\mathrm{rk}^1(u,M)\ge \beta +1$"
(the same definition makes sense even if $\beta =-1$). In particular 

\[
M \models \varphi^M(u)[\dots,a,\dots]_{a\in u}.
\]

\medskip\noindent
We define the forcing notion $\mathbf{P}$. We can put the diagonal
$\{(\eta,\eta):\eta\in {}^\omega 2\}$ into $B$ so we can ignore it. 
We want to produce (in $\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$) a Borel set $B =
\bigcup\limits_{n< \omega} B_n$, each $B_n$ ($\subseteq {}^\omega 2 
\times {}^\omega 2$) closed (in fact perfect), so it is $\lim (T_n)$ 
for some $(2,2)$-tree $T_n$, $B_0$ is the diagonal, and $\bar{\eta}=
\langle \eta_\alpha:\alpha<\mu\rangle$ as witnesses to 
$2^{\aleph_0} \ge \mu$ and such that $\{\eta_\alpha:\alpha < \lambda\}$ gives
the desired square. So for some $2$-place function $g$ from $\lambda$ 
to $\omega$, $\alpha \ne \beta \Rightarrow (\eta_\alpha,\eta_\beta) \in \lim
(T_{g(\alpha,\beta)})$, all this after we force. But we know that we shall have
to use $M$ (by \ref{1.9}). In the forcing our problem will be to prove the
c.c.c. which will be resolved by using $M$ (and rank) in the definition
of the forcing. We shall have a function $f$ which puts the information
on the rank into the trees to help in not having a perfect square. 
Specifically the domain of $f$ is a subset of

\[
\{(u,h):(\exists \ell \in \omega)(u \in [{}^\ell 2]^*) \text{ and }
h:u \times u \rightarrow \omega\}
\] 

\medskip\noindent
(the functions $h$ above are thought of as indexing the $B_n$'s). The
function $f$ will be such that for any distinct 
$\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_{n-1}< \lambda$, if $\langle\eta_{\alpha_t} \restriction \ell:
t<n\rangle$ are pairwise distinct, $u=\{\eta_{\alpha_t} \restriction \ell: t<n\}$,
$h(\eta_{\alpha_t}\restriction \ell,\eta_{\alpha_s}\restriction \ell)
= g(\alpha_t,\alpha_s)$ and $(u,h) \in \mathrm{Dom}(f)$ then 
$\mathrm{rk}^1(\{\alpha_\ell:\ell < n\},M)=f_0(u,h)$ and $f_1(u,h)$ is
$\eta_{\alpha_k}\restriction l$, where $k=k^M(\{\alpha_t:t<n\})$, $f_2(u,h)=
\varphi^M(\{\alpha_\ell:\ell < n\})$ writing the variable as 
$x_\nu$, $\nu\in u$ and $f(u,h)=(f_0(u,h),f_1(u,h),f_2(u,h))$.
(Note: $f$ is a way to say $\bigcup\limits_{n} \lim (T_n)$ contains 
no perfect square; essentially it is equivalent to fixing appropriate 
rank.) All this was to motivate the definition of the forcing notion
$\mathbf{P}$.

A condition $p$ (of $\mathbf{P}$) is an approximation to all this; it consists of:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}[(1)]
\item   $u^p = u[p]$, a finite subset of $\mu$
\smallskip\noindent
\item   $n^p = n[p] < \omega$ and 
$\eta^p_\alpha = \eta_\alpha[p] \in {}^{n[p]}2$ for $\alpha\in u[p]$ 
such that $\alpha \ne \beta \Rightarrow \eta^p_\alpha \ne \eta^p_\beta$.  
To clarify let $t^p_* = \{\eta_\alpha \restriction \ell:\alpha \in u^p,\ell \le n_p\}$ is a full subtree of ${}^{n[p]\ge}2$, i.e. maximal nodes in ${}^{n[p]}2$ only
(not really necessary)\footnote{Added for transparency; it is
  definable from $\langle \eta^p_\alpha : \alpha \in u^p\rangle$; the
  intention was $p \le q \Rightarrow t^p_* = t^1_* \cap {}^{n[p]} 2$
  not stated we may wonder about $(u,h) \in \mathrm{Dom}(f^p),u \subseteq
  2^\ell \wedge u \nsubseteq t^p_*$.  We now exclude them but the
  relation $R$ excludes them (well when we have two members but
  recall $|u| \ge 1000$.  Alternatively demand ${}^{n[p]}2 =
  \{\eta_\alpha \restriction n^p:\alpha \in u^p\}$ which requires a little
  more in some places.}
\smallskip\noindent
\item   $\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^p=\langle m^p_\ell:\ell \le n^p\rangle$ is a strictly
increasing sequence of natural numbers with last element 
$m^p_{n[p]}=m^p=m[p]$. For $m<m[p]$, we have $t^p_m = t_m[p] \subseteq 
\bigcup\limits_{\ell \le n[p]} ({}^\ell 2\times {}^\ell 2)$ 
which is downward closed (i.e., if 
$(\nu_0,\nu_1)\in t^p_m \cap ({}^\ell 2 \times {}^\ell 2)$ 
then $(\nu_0,\nu_1) \restriction k = (\nu_0\restriction k,\nu_1 \restriction k)\in t^p_m$ for all 
$k < \ell$). Also, $(\langle\ \rangle, \langle\ \rangle)\in t^p_m$, and defining $\triangleleft$ naturally we have:
if $(\eta_0,\eta_1)\in t^p_m \cap ({}^\ell 2 \times {}^\ell 2)$ and
$\ell < m^p$ then $$(\exists \nu_0,\nu_1) \big[ (\eta_0,\eta_1)
\triangleleft(\nu_0,\nu_1) \in t^p_m \cap ({}^{\ell+1} 2 
\times {}^{\ell+1} 2) \big].$$

\item   a function $f^p=f[p]$ satisfying:                                     
\begin{enumerate}
    \item its domain is a subset of 
    $$\big\{(u,h) : \exists\ell \le n[p],\  
    u \subseteq t^p_* \subseteq {}^\ell 2,\ |u| \ge 1,\ 
    h : u \times u \to m[p]\big\}$$ such that for all $\eta,\nu \in u$: 
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $h(\eta,\eta) = 0$
        
        \item $\eta \ne \nu \Rightarrow 0 < h(\eta,\nu) < m_\ell^p$ [the upper bound is necessary]
        
        \item $(\eta,\nu) \in t^p_{h(\eta,\nu)}$
    \end{enumerate}
    
    \item $f^p$ is such that 
    $$f^p(u,h) = \big( f^p_0(u,h), f^p_1(u,h), f^p_2(u,h) \big) \in [-1,\alpha(*))
    \times u\times \mathbb{L}_{\omega,\omega}(\tau(M)).$$
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\item   a function $g=g^p$ with domain $\{(\alpha,\beta):\alpha,\beta$ 
from $u^p \cap \lambda\}$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $g(\alpha,\alpha)=0$
    
    \item $\alpha \ne \beta \Rightarrow 0< g (\alpha,\beta) < m^p $
    
    \item $(\eta^p_\alpha,\eta^p_\beta) \in t^p_{g(\alpha,\beta)} \cap 
    ({}^{n(p)} 2 \times {}^{n(p)} 2)$
\end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
\item   $t^p_0=\{(\eta,\eta):\eta \in {}^{n^p \ge}2\}$
\smallskip\noindent
\item \underline{If}  $u \subseteq {}^\ell 2$, $|u| \ge 1$,
$f^p(u,h) = (\beta^*,\rho^*,\varphi^*)$, and $\ell < \ell(*) \le n^p$, 
$e_i$ are functions with domain $u$ (for $i=0,1$) such that 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item For all $\rho \in u$,  
    $\rho \triangleleft e_i(\rho) \in {}^{\ell(*)} 2$ and 
    $e_0(\rho)= e_1(\rho) \Leftrightarrow \rho \ne \rho^*$.
    
    \item $u' =\mathrm{Rang}(e_0 \restriction u) \cup \mathrm{Rang}(e_1 \restriction u)$
    
    \item $h(\eta,\nu) = h'(e_i(\eta),e_i(\nu))$ for $\eta \ne \nu$ in $u$ 
    and $f^p(u',h') = (\beta',\rho',\varphi')$ (so is well defined)
\end{enumerate}
 \underline{then} $\beta' < \beta^*$  
\smallskip\noindent
\item \underline{If} $\ell \le n^p$, $w \subseteq u^p \cap \lambda$ 
is nonempty, the sequence $\langle \eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell:\alpha \in w \rangle$ 
is with no repetitions and $h$ is defined by 
$h(\eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell,\eta^p_\beta \restriction \ell) = g^p(\alpha,\beta)$ 
for $\alpha \ne \beta$ from $w$ (and $h(\eta^p_\alpha \restriction
\ell,\eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell) = 0$) and $u = \{\eta^p_\alpha \restriction
\ell: \alpha \in w\}$, \underline{then} $f^p(u,h)$ is well-defined hence
$[\alpha \ne \beta \in u \Rightarrow g(\alpha,\beta) < m^p_\ell]$, 
$f^p_2(u,h) = \varphi^M(w)$, $f^p_1(u,h) = \eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell$ where 
$\alpha$ is the $k^M(w)$-th member of $w$ and
$f^p_0(u,h)=\mathrm{rk}^1(w,M)$; of course in $f^p_2(u, h)=\varphi^M(w)$ the
variable $x_\nu$ in $f^p_2(u, h)$ corresponds to $x_{|\alpha\cap w|}$ if
$\eta_\alpha \restriction l=\nu$ (see last clause of $\oplus_p$ below)
\smallskip\noindent
\item   if $(u,h) \in \mathrm{Dom}(f^p)$ \underline{then} for some $w$ and 
$\ell$, $f^p(u,h)$ is obtained as in clause (8)
\smallskip\noindent
\item   if $\eta_1 \ne \eta_2$ are in ${}^\ell 2$, $\ell \le n^p$ and
$(\eta_1,\eta_2)\in t^p_m$, $0 < m < m^p$ \underline{then} for some
$\alpha_1 \ne \alpha_2$ from $u^p \cap \lambda$ we have 
$g^p(\alpha_1,\alpha_2) = m$ and $\eta_1 \trianglelefteq \eta^p_{\alpha_1}$, 
$\eta_2 \trianglelefteq \eta^p_{\alpha_2}$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
{\bf The order} is the natural one (including the following
requirements:  $p \le q$ \underline{iff} ($p,q \in \mathbf{P}$ and) $n^p \le n^q$, 
$m^p \le m^q$, $\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^p = \mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^q \restriction (n^p+1)$, $u^p \subseteq u^q$, 
$\eta^q_\alpha \restriction n^p = \eta^p_\alpha$ for $\alpha\in u^p$, 
$t^p_* = t^1_* \cap {}^{n[p] \ge} 2$, $t^p_m=t^q_m\cap \bigcup\limits_{\ell \le n[p]} 
({}^\ell 2 \times {}^\ell 2)$ for $m < m^p$, $g^p = g^q \restriction u^p$ 
and $f^p = f^q \restriction \{(u,h) \in \mathrm{Dom}(f^q) : u \subseteq {}^{n^p\ge}2\}$, 
so if $(u,h) \notin \mathrm{Dom}(f^p)$, $u \subseteq{}^{n^p\ge}2$ 
then $(u,h) \notin \mathrm{Dom}(f^q)$).
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
The function $f^p$ of a condition $p \in \mathbf{P}$ carries 
no additional information. It is determined by the function $g^p$ and 
functions $\varphi^M$, $k^M$ and the rank. Conditions 8, 9 are to say
that:
\medskip\noindent 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$\oplus_p$]   \underline{If} $w_0,w_1 \subseteq \lambda\cap u^p$,
$\ell \le n^p$, $u = \{\eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell:\alpha \in w_0\} =
\{\eta^p_\alpha\restriction \ell : \alpha \in w_1\}$ (no repetitions) are 
non empty and $h : u\times u \to m^p$ is such that if 
either $\alpha,\beta \in w_0$ or $\alpha,\beta \in w_1$ then 
$h(\eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell,\eta^p_\beta \restriction \ell) = g^p(\alpha,\beta)$
\underline{then} $\mathrm{rk}^1(w_0,M) = \mathrm{rk}^1(w_1,M)$, $\varphi^M(w_0)=\varphi^M(w_1)$, 
$k^M(w_0) = k^M(w_1)$, and if $\alpha_i$, $\beta_i \in w_i$ for $i=0,1$ and
$\eta_{\alpha_0}\restriction \ell =\eta_{\alpha_1}\restriction \ell$,
$\eta_{\beta_0}\restriction \ell = \eta_{\beta_1}\restriction \ell$ then
$\alpha_0 < \alpha_1 \Leftrightarrow \beta_0 < \beta_1$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
Moreover, condition 7 gives no additional restriction unless
$f^p_0(u,h)=-1$. Indeed, suppose that $u\subseteq {}^\ell 2$, $|u| \ge 1$,
$\ell < \ell(*) \le n^p$, $e_i:u \rightarrow {}^{\ell(*)}2$, 
$h,\rho^* \in u$, $u'$ and $h'$ are as there and 
$f^p_0(u,h) \ge 0$.   As $f^p(u',h')$ is defined we find $w \subseteq
\lambda \cap u^p$, $\alpha_0,\alpha_1 \in w$ ($\alpha_0 \ne \alpha_1$) 
such that $u'=\{\eta^p_\alpha \restriction l(*) : \alpha\in w\}$,
$h'(\eta^p_\alpha \restriction \ell(*)$, 
$\eta^p_\beta \restriction \ell(*)) = g^p(\alpha,\beta) < m^p_l$ and 
$e_i(\rho^*) = \eta^p_{\alpha_i} \restriction \ell(*)$ (for $i=0,1$). Looking at
$w \setminus\{\alpha_0\}$, $w \setminus\{\alpha_1\}$ and $(u,h)$ we see that
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{1.11}
Note that if $B$ is a subset of the plane $(^\omega\omega,
{}^\omega\omega)$ which is $G_\delta$ (i.e. $\bigcap\limits_{n<\omega}
U_n$, $U_n$ open, without loss of generality decreasing with $n$) and it
contains an uncountable square $X\times X$ (so $X\subseteq
{}^\omega\omega$ is uncountable) {\it then} it contains a perfect
square. Why? 

Let

\[
X' = \big\{\eta \in X: (\forall n)(\exists^{\aleph_1}
\nu)[\nu\in X \text{ and } \nu \restriction n = \eta\restriction n]\big\}.
\]

\medskip\noindent

Let

\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{clcr}
K = \{(u,n): &\text{for some } \ell = \ell(u,n),\ u \subseteq
{}^\ell \omega,\ \eta,\nu \in u, \\
   & \eta \triangleleft \eta' \in {}^\omega \omega \text{ and } \nu \triangleleft \nu' \in {}^\omega \omega \Rightarrow (\eta',\nu') \in U_n \\
   &\text{ and } \eta \in u \text{ and } \eta \triangleleft \eta' \in
{}^\omega \omega \Rightarrow (\eta',\eta') \in U_n\}
\end{array}
\end{equation*}

\[
K' = \{(u,n)\in K: \text{ for some } \bar \nu=\langle \nu_\rho:\rho\in
u\rangle \text{ we have } \nu_\rho \in X',\ \rho \triangleleft
\nu_\rho\}.
\]

\medskip\noindent
So
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)]  $K' \ne \varnothing$, in fact if $\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_m \in X'$
    are pairwise distinct, $n< \omega$, then for any $\ell$ large enough
    $(\{\eta_i\restriction \ell: i=1, \ldots, m\}, n)\in K$,
\smallskip\noindent
    \item[(b)]  if $(u,n) \in K'$ as exemplified by 
    $\bar \nu = \langle \nu_\rho : \rho \in u\rangle$ and $\rho^*\in u$, 
    $\nu'\in X'\setminus \{\nu_{\rho^*}\}$, 
    $\nu' \restriction \ell = \nu_{\rho^*} \restriction \ell$ {\it then} for any 
    $\ell' \in (\ell, \omega)$ and $n' > n$ large enough, we have 
    $\big(\{\nu_\rho \restriction \ell' : \rho \in u\} \cup \{\nu'\restriction \ell'\}, n'\big) \in K'$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{1.12}
Assume MA and $2^{\aleph_0} \ge \lambda_{\omega_1}(\aleph_0)$ 
or $2^{\aleph_0}>\mu$.  
Then:\ there is a Borel subset of the plane with a $\mu$-square but with no
perfect square \underline{iff} $\mu<\lambda_{\omega_1}(\aleph_0)$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
he first clause implies the second clause by \ref{1.9}. If the second
clause holds, let $\mu\leq \lambda_\alpha(\aleph_0)$ and $\alpha<
\omega_1$, by \ref{3.2}(6) letting 
$\eta_i\in {}^\omega 2$ for $i< \mu$ be pairwise distinct we can find an
$\omega$-sequence of $(2, 2)$-trees $\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$ such that $(\eta_i,
\eta_j)\in \bigcup\limits_n (\lim T_n)$ for $i, j<\mu$ and
$\mathrm{degsq} (\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\alpha$ (just use $A=\{(\eta_i, \eta_j): i, j<\mu\}$
there). By
\ref{3.2}(3) the set $\bigcup\limits_{n} (\lim T_n)$ contains no
$\lambda_{\alpha+1}(\aleph_0)$-square.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- fact-1: body_md -->
\label{1.13}
Assume $\mathbf{P}$ is adding $\mu > \kappa$ Cohen reals or random 
reals and $\kappa > 2^{\aleph_0}$.

\underline{Then} in $\mathbf{V}^\mathbf{P}$ we have:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_\kappa$]  there is no Borel set (or analytic) $B\subseteq
  {}^\omega 2 \times {}^\omega 2$ such that:
\smallskip\noindent 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]   there are $\eta_\alpha \in {}^\omega 2$ for $\alpha
  <\kappa$ such that $[\alpha \ne \beta$ implies $\eta_\alpha \ne 
\eta_\beta]$, and $(\eta_\alpha,\eta_\beta) \in B$ for $\alpha,\beta <
\kappa$
\smallskip\noindent 
\item[(b)]  $B$ contains no perfect square.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end fact-1 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
traight as in the (last) stage G of the proof of theorem
\ref{1.10} (except that no relevance of (7) of Stage A there).

Let $\mathbf{P}$ be adding $\langle \let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3r$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\alpha: \alpha< \mu\rangle$,
assume $p \in \mathbf{P}$ forces that: $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3B$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ a Borel set,
$\langle \let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\eta$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\alpha: \alpha < \kappa\rangle$ are as in clause
(a), (b) above. Let $\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\eta$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\alpha$ be names in
$\mathbf{P}_{v_\alpha}= \mathbf{P} \restriction \{\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3r$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\beta: 
\beta\in v_\alpha\}$, and $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3B$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ be a name in $\mathbf{P}_v = 
\mathbf{P}\restriction \{\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3r$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\beta: \beta\in v\}$ where $v$, 
$v_\beta$ are countable subsets of $\kappa$.
Without loss of generality, $\langle v_\alpha: \alpha< (2^{\aleph_0})^+\rangle$ is 
a $\Delta$-system with heart $v$ and 
$\mathrm{otp}(v_\alpha \setminus v)=\mathrm{otp}(v_0\setminus v)$. In
$\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{P}_v}$ we have $\let\theaccent\widetilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3B$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ and 
$2^{\aleph_0}= (2^{\aleph_0})^\mathbf{V}$,
so without loss of generality $v = \varnothing$ and $\mathrm{otp}(v_\alpha)$ does not depend on $\alpha$.

Without loss of generality the order preserving function 
$f_{\alpha,\beta}$ from $v_\alpha$ onto $v_\beta$ maps 
$\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\eta$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\alpha$ to $\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\eta$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_\beta$. So for
$Q$=Cohen in the Cohen case we have a name $\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\tau$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}$ such that 
$\Vdash_{\mathrm{Cohen}} ``\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\tau$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}(\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3r$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth})\in {}^\omega 2$
is new", $\Vdash_{\mathrm{Cohen} \times \mathrm{Cohen}} ``(\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\tau$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}(\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3r$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_1);
\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3\tau$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}(\let\theaccent\tilde\skewfactor-
\mathpalette\oalign{$3r$\crcr\hidewidth
\vbox to.2ex{\hbox{$3\skew\skewfactor\theaccent{}$}\vss}\hidewidth}_2))\in B$", and we can finish easily. The random
case is similar.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- conclusion-3: body_md -->
\label{1.14}
1) For $\kappa \in (\aleph_1,\aleph_{\omega_1})$ the statement 
$(*)_\kappa$ of \ref{1.13} is not decided by 
$\mathrm{ZFC} +2^{\aleph_0}>\aleph_{\omega_1}$ (i.e. it and its
negation are consistent with ZFC).

\noindent
2)  \ref{1.13} applies to the forcing notion of \ref{1.10} (with $\mu$
instead of $2^{\aleph_0}$).
<!-- end conclusion-3 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
) Starting with universe $\mathbf{V}$ satisfying $\mathrm{CH}$, Fact \ref{1.13} shows the
consistency of ``yes". As by \ref{1.6}(1) we know that
$\lambda_{\omega_1}(\aleph_0) \ge \aleph_{\omega_1}$ \underline{and}
$\aleph_{\omega_1}> \kappa$ (by assumption), Theorem \ref{1.12}
(with the classical consistency of $\mathrm{MA} + 2^{\aleph_0}> 
\aleph_{\omega_1}$) gives the consistency of ``no" (in fact in both
cases it works for all $\kappa$ simultaneously).

\noindent
2) Left to the reader.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- claim-6: body_md -->
\label{2.1}
The following are equivalent for a cardinal $\lambda$.

\noindent
1)  $\Pr_{\omega_1}(\lambda)$.

\noindent
2)  If $\psi\in L_{\omega_{1},\omega}$ has a model $M$ with $|R^M|\ge \lambda$
($R$ is a unary predicate) {\it then} $\psi$ has a model of the cardinality
continuum, moreover for some countable first order theory $\mathbf{T}_1$ 
with Skolem functions such that $\tau(\psi)\subseteq\tau(\mathbf{T}_1)$ and a
model $M_1$ of $\mathbf{T}_1$ and $a_\eta \in R^{M_1}$ for $\eta \in
{}^\omega 2$ we have:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(*)_0$]   $M_1 \models \psi$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(*)_1$]  $M_1$, $a_\eta$ ($\eta \in {}^\omega 2$) are as in 
\cite[Ch.II,\S4]{Sh:a} = \cite[Ch.VII,\S4]{Sh:c}, i.e.:
\smallskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)]   $M_1$ is the Skolem hull of $\{a_\eta:\eta \in {}^\omega
  2\}$ and $\eta \ne \nu$ implies $a_\eta \ne a_\nu$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[{(b)}]  for every $n < \omega$ and a first order formula
  $\varphi = \varphi(x_0,\dots,x_{n-1}) \in L(\mathbf{T}_1)$ there is 
$n^*< \omega$ such that: for every $k \in (n^*,\omega),\eta_0,\dots,
\eta_{n-1} \in {}^\omega 2$ and $\nu_0,\dots,\nu_{n-1} \in {}^\omega
2$  satisfying $\bigwedge\limits_{m<n} \eta_m \restriction k=\nu_m \restriction k 
\text{ and } \bigwedge\limits_{m < \ell <n}\eta_m \restriction k \ne
\eta_\ell \restriction k$ we have 
$M_1 \models ``\varphi [a_{\eta_0},\dots,a_{\eta_{n-1}}] \equiv \varphi
[a_{\nu_0},\dots,a_{\nu_{n-1}}]"$.
Note that necessarily $a_\eta \notin \mathrm{Skolem\ Hull}_{M_1}\{a_\nu: \nu\in
{}^\omega 2\setminus \{\eta\}\}$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[(c)]   $a_\eta\in R^{M_1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end claim-6 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
We can prove similarly with replacing $\lambda$ by ``for arbitrarily
large $\lambda'< \lambda$" here and elsewhere; i.e. in 2) we replace the
assumption by ``If $\psi\in L_{\omega_1, \omega}$ has, for every
$\lambda' <\lambda$, a model $M$ with $|R^M|\ge \lambda'$ \underline{then}
$\ldots$" (and still the new version of 2) is equivalent to 1)).
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
\underline{$1 \Rightarrow 2$}

\noindent 
Just as in \cite{Sh:37}+ \cite{Sh:49}: without loss of generality $\|M\|=\lambda$ and
moreover $|M|=\lambda$.
Let $M_1$ be an expansion of $M$ by names for subformulas of $\psi$, a
pairing function, and then by Skolem functions. Let $\mathbf{T}_1$ be the first
order theory of $M_1$. There is (see \cite{Ke71}) a set $\Gamma$ of
countably many types 
$p(x)$ such that: $M_1$ omits every $p(x)\in \Gamma$ and if
$M'_1$ is a model of $\mathbf{T}_1$ omitting every $p(x)\in \Gamma$ then
$M'_1$ is a model of $\psi$ (just for each subformula
$\bigwedge\limits_{n<\omega} \psi_n(\bar x)$ of $\psi$, we have to
omit a type; we can use 1-types as we have a pairing function).

Let us define
$Y= \{v\subseteq {}^{\omega >}2:v$ is finite nonempty and its members are
pairwise $\triangleleft$-incomparable and for some $n$, 
$v\subseteq {}^n 2\cup {}^{n+1}2\}$, $Z = \big\{(v, \varphi(\dots,x_\eta,\dots)_{\eta\in v}) : v \in Y,\ \varphi$ a formula in $T_1$ with the set of free variables included in
$\{x_\eta : \eta\in v\}$ and for every $\alpha < \omega_1$ 
there are $a^\alpha_\eta \in R^M$ for $\eta \in v$ such that 
$[\eta \ne \nu \in v \Rightarrow a_\eta^\alpha \ne a_\nu^\alpha]$ 
and $\mathrm{rk}^0(\{a^\alpha_\eta :\eta \in v\},M)\ge \alpha$ and $M 
\models \varphi[\ldots,a^\alpha_\eta,\ldots]_{\eta\in v}\big\}$.  

We say for $(v_\ell,\varphi_\ell) \in Z$ ($\ell = 1,2$) that
<$(v_2,\varphi_2) \in \mathrm{suc} (v_1,\varphi_1)$ if for some $\eta \in v_1$ 
(called $\eta(v_1,v_2)$) we have
$v_2=(v_1 \backslash \{\eta\}) \cup \{\eta {\char 94} \langle 0\rangle,
\eta {\char 94} \langle 1\rangle\}$ and letting for $i<2$ the 
function $h_i:v_1 \rightarrow v_2$ be $h_i(\nu)$ is $\nu$ if $\nu \ne 
\eta$ and it is $\eta {\char 94} \langle i\rangle$ if $\nu =\eta$, 
we demand for $i=0,1$:

\[
\varphi_2 \vdash \varphi_1 (\dots,x_{h_i(\nu)},\dots)_{\nu \in v_1}.
\]

\medskip\noindent
Choose inductively $\langle (v_\ell,\varphi_\ell):\ell < \omega
\rangle$ such that $(v_{\ell+1},\varphi_{\ell+1}) \in 
\mathrm{suc}(v_\ell,\varphi_\ell)$ is generic enough, i.e.:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$\otimes_1$]  if $\varphi = \varphi(x_0,\ldots,x_{k-1})
\in L(\mathbf{T}_1)$ then for some $\ell < \omega$ 
for every $m\in [\ell,\omega)$ and $\eta_0,\ldots,\eta_{k-1} \in v_m$ we have:
$\varphi_m \vdash \varphi(x_{\eta_0},\ldots,x_{\eta_{k-1}})$ or 
$\varphi_m \vdash \neg\varphi(x_{\eta_0},\ldots,x_{\eta_{k-1}})$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$\otimes_2$]  for every $p(x)\in \Gamma$ and for every function
symbol $f=f(x_0, \ldots,x_{n-1})$ (note: in $\mathbf{T}_1$ definable function
is equivalent to some function symbol), for some $\ell < \omega$ for every
$m \in [\ell,\omega)$, for every $\eta_0,\ldots,\eta_{n_1} \in v_m$ for
some $\psi(x)\in p(x)$ we have
$\varphi_m \vdash \neg \psi(f(x_{\eta_0}),\ldots,f(x_{\eta_{n-1}}))$.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
It is straightforward to carry the induction (to simplify you may
demand in $(\otimes)_1$, $(\otimes)_2$ 
just ``for arbitrarily large $m \in [\ell,\omega)"$,
this does not matter and the stronger version of $(\otimes)_1,
(\otimes)_2$ can be gotten (replacing the $^{\omega>}2$ by a 
perfect subtree $T$ and then renaming
$a_\eta$ for $\eta\in \lim(T)$ as $a_\eta$ for $\eta\in {}^\omega 2$)).
Then define the model by the compactness.
\medskip

\noindent 
\underline{$2 \Rightarrow 1$}:

If not, then $\mathrm{NPr}_{\omega_1}(\lambda)$ hence for some model $M$ with
vocabulary $\tau$, $|\tau|\le \aleph_0$, cardinality $\lambda$ we have
$\alpha(*) := \mathrm{rk}^0(M)< \omega_1$.  Let $\psi_{\alpha(*)} \in
L_{\omega_1,\omega}(\tau)$ be as in \ref{2.2} below, so necessarily $M\models
\psi_{\alpha(*)}$. Apply to it clause (2) which holds by our present
assumption (with $R^M=\lambda$), so $\psi_{\alpha(*)}$ has a model
$M_1$ as there, (so $M_1 \models \psi_{\alpha(*)})$. 
But $\{a_\eta:\eta\in {}^\omega 2\}$ easily witnesses
$\mathrm{rk}^0(M_1)=\infty$, moreover, for every nonempty finite $w \subseteq
\{a_\eta:\eta\in {}^\omega 2\}$ and an ordinal 
$\alpha$ we have $\mathrm{rk}^0(w,M)\ge \alpha$. This
can be easily proved by induction on $\alpha$ (using $(*)_2$(b) of (2) (and
$\eta\neq \nu\in {}^\omega 2 \Rightarrow a_\eta \ne a_\nu$ of
$(*)_2$(a))).
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- fact-2: body_md -->
\label{2.2}
1)  For every $\alpha<\kappa^+$ and vocabulary $\tau$, 
$|\tau| \le \kappa$, there  is a sentence 
$\psi_\alpha \in L_{\kappa^+,\omega}[\tau]$ (of quantifier depth $\alpha$) 
such that for any $\tau$-model $M$:  

\[
M \models \psi_\alpha \text{ \underline{iff} } \mathrm{rk}^0(M; <\aleph_0)=\alpha.
\]


\medskip\noindent
2)  For every $\alpha< \theta^+$, $\ell \in \{0, 1\}$ and
vocabulary $\tau$, $|\tau|\le \theta$ there is a sentence 
$\psi \in L_{\theta^+, \omega}(\exists^{\ge \kappa})[\tau]$ 
($\exists^{\ge \kappa}$ is the quantifier ``there are $\ge \kappa$ many $x$-s'') 
such that for any $\tau$-model $M$, $M\models \psi^\ell_\alpha$
\underline{iff} $\mathrm{rk}^\ell(M; <\kappa,\theta)=\alpha$.
<!-- end fact-2 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
asy to check.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- conclusion-4: body_md -->
\label{2.3}
Assume $\tau$ is a countable vocabulary.
 If $\psi\in L_{\omega_1,\omega}(\tau)$, $R$ is a unary predicate,
 $\tau_0\subseteq \tau$, $\Delta \subseteq \{\varphi(x):\varphi 
\in L_{\omega_1,\omega}(\tau_0)\}$ is 
countable and for some transitive model $\mathbf{V}_1$ of ZFC (may be a generic
extension of $\mathbf{V}$ or an inner model as long as $\psi$, $\Delta
\in \mathbf{V}_1$ and $\mathbf{V}_1 \models ``\psi \in
L_{\omega_1,\omega}(\tau),\ \Delta \subseteq \{\varphi(x) : \varphi \in 
L_{\omega_1,\omega}(\tau_0)\}"$) we have
$\mathbf{V}_1 \models ``\Pr_{\omega_1}(\lambda)$ and $\psi$ has a 
model $M$ with $\lambda \le \big|\big\{\{\varphi(x) : M \models \varphi[a],\ 
\varphi(x) \in \Delta\} : a \in R^M \big\}\big|"$.

\underline{Then} we can find a model $N$ of $\psi$ with Skolem functions and  
$a_\alpha \in R^N$ for $\alpha < 2^{\aleph_0}$ such that 
for each $\alpha<2^{\aleph_0}$ the type
$p_\alpha = \{\pm\varphi(x):N \models \pm \varphi[a_\alpha]$ 
and $\varphi(x)\in \Delta\}$ is not realized in the Skolem hull of

\[
\{a_\beta:\beta < 2^{\aleph_0} \text{ and } \beta \ne \alpha\}.
\]

\medskip\noindent
Hence $\big|\{M/{\approx} : M\models \psi,\ \|M\|=\lambda\}\big|\ge 
\min\{2^\lambda,\beth_2\}$ (really here we should say\\ $(M \restriction \tau_0)/{\approx}$).
                                                                                                                      
Moreover we can find such a family of models no one of them 
                                    
we have embeddable into another by an embedding preserving 
$\pm\varphi(x)$ for $\varphi \in\Delta$.
<!-- end conclusion-4 -->
<!-- claim-7: body_md -->
\label{2.4}
1) For cardinals $\lambda > \kappa \ge \aleph_0$ the following are
equivalent:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)]   $\Pr_{\kappa^+}(\lambda;\kappa)$
\smallskip\noindent
    \item[(b)]   If $M$ is a model, $\tau(M)$ countable, $R,R_0 \in \tau(M)$ 
    unary predicates, $|R^M_0| \le \kappa$, $\lambda \le |R^M|$ \underline{then} we can find $M_0, M_1,a_\eta (\eta\in {}^\omega 2)$ such that:
\smallskip\noindent
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item[(i)]   $M_1$ is a model of the (first order) universal theory of $M$
        (and is a $\tau(M)$-model)  
\smallskip\noindent
        \item[(ii)]  $a_\eta \in R^{M_1}$ for $\eta \in {}^\omega 2$ 
        are pairwise distinct   
\smallskip\noindent
        \item[(iii)]   $M_1$ is the closure of 
        $\{a_\eta:\eta \in {}^\omega 2\}\cup M_0$ under the functions of $M_1$ (so 
\smallskip\noindent
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item[$(\alpha)$]  $M_1$ also includes the individual constants of $M$; in
            general $\|M_1\|=2^{\aleph_0}$
\smallskip\noindent
            \item[$(\beta)$]  if $\tau(M)$ has predicates only then
            $|M_1| = \{a_\eta:\eta \in {}^\omega 2\} \cup |M_0|$)
        \end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
        \item[(iv)]   $M_0$ is countable, $M_0\subseteq M$, $M_0\subseteq M_1$,
        $M_0 = c \ell_M(M_0\cap R^M_0)$, $R^{M_1}_0=R^{M_0}_0(\subseteq R^M_0)$. 
        In fact, we can have:  
\smallskip\noindent
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item[$(*)$]   $(M_1,c)_{c \in M_0}$ is a model of the universal theory of
            $(M,c)_{c\in M_0}$ 
        \end{enumerate}
\smallskip\noindent
        \item[(v)]   for every $n< \omega$ and a quantifier free first order 
        formula $\varphi = \varphi(x_0,\dots, x_{n-1}) \in L(\tau(M))$ 
        \underline{there is} $n^*< \omega$ such that: 
        for every $k \in (n^*,\omega)$ and $\eta_0,\dots,\eta_{n-1} \in
        {}^\omega 2,\nu_0,\dots, \nu_{n-1}\in {}^\omega 2$ satisfying  
        $\bigwedge\limits_{m<n} \eta_m \restriction k = \nu_m \restriction k$ and 
        $\bigwedge\limits_{m <\ell < n} \eta_m \restriction k \ne \eta_\ell \restriction k$
        we have $M_1 \models ``\varphi[a_{\eta_0},\dots,a_{\eta_{n-1}}] \equiv 
        \varphi[a_{\nu_0},\dots,a_{\nu_{n-1}}]"$, we can even allow parameters 
        from $M_0$ in $\varphi$ (but $k$ depends on them).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate} 
\medskip\noindent
2)  For cardinals $\lambda>\kappa\geq \aleph_0$ the following are
equivalent:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(a)$'$]  $\Pr_{\omega_1}(\lambda; \kappa)$
\smallskip\noindent
    \item[(b)$'$]  like (b) above, but we omit $``M_0 \subseteq M"$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end claim-7 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{2.4A}
1)  See \ref{4.4}, \ref{4.5} how to use claim \ref{2.4}.

\noindent
2)  In (b), if $M$ has Skolem functions then we 
automatically get also: 
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(i)$^+$]   $M_1$ a model of the first order theory of $M$
\smallskip\noindent
    \item[(iii)$^+$]  $M_1$ is the Skolem hull of 
    $\{a_\eta : \eta \in {}^\omega 2\} \cup M_0$
\smallskip\noindent
    \item[(iv)$^+$]  $M_0 \prec M$, $M_0 \prec M_1$, $M_0$ countable 
    (and $R^{M_1}_0 = R^{M_0}_0 \subseteq R^M_0$)
\smallskip\noindent
    \item[(v)$^+$]  clause {\bf (v)} above holds even for $\varphi$ 
    any (first order) formula of $L_{\omega,\omega}(\tau(M))$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
) \underline{$(a) \Rightarrow (b)$}
 
Like the proof of \ref{2.1}, $(1) \Rightarrow (2)$, applied to $(M,c)_{c\in
R^M_0}$ but the set $M_0\cap R^M_0$ is chosen by finite approximation
i.e. (letting $Y$ be as there and $\tau=\tau(M)$) we let
$Z = \big\{(v,\varphi(\ldots, x_\eta, \ldots)_{\eta\in v}, A): v\in Y$, 
$\varphi$ a quantifier free formula in $L_{\omega,\omega}(\tau)$ with 
set of free variables included in $\{x_\eta:\eta\in v\}$
and parameters from $A$. $A$ is a finite subset of $R^M_0$,
 and for every ordinal $\alpha< \kappa^+$ there are 
 $a^\alpha_\eta \in R^M$ for $\eta\in v$ such that $[\eta \ne \nu$ from 
 $v \Rightarrow a^{\alpha}_\eta \ne a^{\alpha}_\nu]$ and 
 $\mathrm{rk}(\{a^\alpha_\eta : \eta\in v\},M) \ge \alpha$ and 
 $M \models \varphi[\ldots,a^\alpha_\eta,\ldots]_{\eta\in v}\big\}$. 

We need here the ``for every $\alpha< \kappa^+$" because we want to fix
elements of $R^M_0$, and there are possibly $\kappa$ choices.

\noindent
\underline{$\neg (a) \Rightarrow \neg (b)$}:

Like the proof of \ref{2.2}; assume
$\mathrm{NPr}_\alpha (\lambda; \kappa)$, $\alpha< \kappa^+$, let $M$
witness it, choose $R_0=\alpha+1$, $R=\lambda$, without loss of generality $\tau
(M)=\{R_{n,\zeta} : n < \omega,\ \zeta < \kappa\}$, $R_{n,\zeta}$ is
$n$-place, in $M$ every quantifier free formula is equivalent 
to some $R_{n,\zeta}$. Let $R^*_{n, k}
:= \Big\{(i_0,\ldots,i_{n-1},\beta,\zeta): M \models
R_{n, \zeta }(i_0, \ldots ,i_{n-1})$, $\{i_0, \ldots , i_{n-1}\}$ is
with no repetition, increasing for simplicity,
and $\mathrm{rk}(\{ i_0, \ldots, i_{n-1}\}, M;
\kappa)=\beta$, with $\mathrm{rk}(\{i_0, \ldots, i_{n-1}\}, M; \kappa) \not\ge
\beta+1$ being witnessed by $\varphi(\{i_0, \ldots, i_{n-1}\})=R_{n, \zeta}$,
$k(\{i_0,\ldots, i_{n-1}\})=k\Big\}$ where the functions $\varphi$, $k$ are
as in the proof of \ref{1.10}. Let $M$ be $(\lambda, <, R, R_0,
R^*_{n,k})_{n\in (0, \omega), k<n}$ expanded by Skolem functions.
So assume toward contradiction that (b) holds, hence for this model $M$
there are models $M_0$, $M_1$ and $a_\eta\in M_1$ for $\eta\in {}^\omega
2$ as required in clauses (i) -- (v) of (b) of claim \ref{2.4}. Choose a
non-empty finite subset $w$ of $^\omega 2$ and $\beta$ and $\zeta$ such
that letting $w=\{\eta_0, \ldots, \eta_{m-1}\}$ with $a_{\eta_\ell}
<^{M_1} a_{\eta_{\ell+1}}$, we have:
\medskip\noindent
\begin{enumerate}
\item[$(\alpha)$]  $M_1 \models R^*_{m,k}(a_{\eta_0},\ldots,a_{\eta_{m-1}},
\beta, \zeta)$
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\beta)$]   $\beta\in R^{M_0}_0$ ($\subseteq \alpha$)
\smallskip\noindent
\item[$(\gamma)$] $\beta$ minimal under those constraints.
\end{enumerate}
\medskip\noindent
Note that there are $m$, $\eta_0, \ldots, \eta_{m-1}$, $\beta$, $\zeta$ such
that $(\alpha)$ holds: for every non-empty $w\subseteq {}^\omega 2$, as $M_1$
is elementarily equivalent to $M$ there are $\beta$, $\zeta$ as required
in $(\alpha)$.
Now $(\alpha)$ implies $\zeta\in R^{M_1}_0$, but $R^{M_1}_0 = R^{M_0}_0$,
so clause
$(\beta)$ holds too, and so we can satisfy $(\gamma)$ too as the ordinals
are well ordered. Let $\varphi'=\varphi(x_0, \ldots, x_{m-1}, \beta, \zeta)$,
note the parameters are from $R^{M_1}_0$ (as $M_1$ is elementarily
equivalent to $M$) hence from $R^{M_0}_0\subseteq M$, and clause (v) (of
(b) of \ref{2.4}) applies to $\varphi'$, $\langle \eta_0, \ldots,
\eta_{m-1}\rangle$ giving $n^*< \omega$. We can find $\eta'_k\in
{}^\omega 2$, $\eta'_k \neq \eta_{k}$, $\eta'_k \restriction n^* =
\eta_k \restriction n^*$, and easily for $w'=\{\eta_, \ldots,
\eta_{m-1}, \eta'_k\}$ we can find $\beta' < \beta$, $\zeta'< \kappa$
and $k$ such that $M_1\models R_{m+1, k'} (a_{\eta_0}, \ldots,
a_{\eta_{m-1}}, a_{\eta'_k}, \beta', \zeta')$ and then if $\beta
